# Starting project at /workspaces/alarmG-native

ERROR An unknown error occurred while installing React Native DevTools. Details:

/workspaces/alarmG-native/node_modules/@react-native/debugger-shell/bin/react-native-devtools: error while loading shared libraries: libatk-1.0.so.0: cannot open shared object file: No such file or directory

Starting Metro Bundler

warning: Bundler cache is empty, rebuilding (this may take a minute)
CommandError: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'body')
Check the Ngrok status page for outages: [https://status.ngrok.com/](https://status.ngrok.com/)

This is unrelated to your code — the `libatk-1.0.so.0` line is the same harmless DevTools warning from before, ignore it. The real error is:

```text
CommandError: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'body')
Check the Ngrok status page for outages
```

That's the `--tunnel` flag's underlying ngrok connection failing to establish — a network/service issue between Codespaces and ngrok's servers, not a bug in `RingingScreen.tsx` or `SetupScreen.tsx`.

## Quick retry first

```bash
git status
```

Just confirm nothing's mid-edit, then:

```bash
npx expo start --tunnel --clear
```

Free ngrok tunnels are flaky and this often resolves itself on a second attempt.

## If it fails again

```bash
rm -rf node_modules/@expo/ngrok
eas login
npx expo start --tunnel --clear
```

Removing the ngrok folder forces a fresh reinstall of the tunnel binary; logging in sometimes stabilizes it too.

## If it's still failing — skip it and build the APK instead

You need `eas login` anyway for the APK build, and the APK **doesn't use ngrok or the tunnel at all** — it connects directly once installed. Rather than fighting a flaky free tunnel service, let's just move forward:

```bash
npx tsc --noEmit
```

Run that first — it only checks TypeScript locally, no network/tunnel needed. If it comes back clean with no output, your code is fine regardless of the tunnel issue. Paste the output here.

Then, tunnel or not, proceed straight to:

```bash
eas login
eas build:configure
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```

This builds in Expo's cloud (unaffected by your Codespaces' ngrok problem) and gives you a download link/QR for a real installable APK — which tests everything (sound, haptics, camera, snooze PIN, barcode) on your actual phone without needing Expo Go or a tunnel ever again.

Send me the `tsc` output first, then tell me if the tunnel retry worked or if you want to just go straight to the build.

# all in one go?eas login

eas build:configure
eas build --platform android --profile preview or one by one\`\` and ain code spaces right
?

Run them one by one, not all at once — each command needs you to respond to prompts before the next one makes sense. And yes, all of this happens in the same Codespaces terminal you've been using.

## Run these in order, waiting for each to finish

### 1. Log in

```bash
eas login
```

It will ask for your Expo account email/username and password. If you don't have an Expo account yet, go to `expo.dev` in a browser tab, sign up free, then come back and run this.

### 2. Configure the project

```bash
eas build:configure
```

It will ask:

```text
Which platforms would you like to configure?
```

Select **Android** (or "All" if it only gives that option).

It may also ask to auto-detect or confirm the Android package name — it should read `com.rainbowblurry.wakeupnative` from your `app.json` and just confirm it. Say yes/accept.

This creates a new file: `eas.json`.

### 3. Check `eas.json` before building

Open the new `eas.json` file and make sure the `preview` profile looks like this:

```json
"preview": {
  "distribution": "internal",
  "android": {
    "buildType": "apk"
  }
}
```

If `eas build:configure` created a `preview` profile without `"buildType": "apk"`, add that line — otherwise it may output a Play Store `.aab` file instead of an installable `.apk`. Save if you changed anything.

### 4. Build

```bash
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```

This uploads your project to Expo's cloud build servers and compiles it there — nothing runs locally, no tunnel involved. It'll show progress in the terminal and take roughly 10–20 minutes.

When it finishes, it prints a QR code and a URL. Scan the QR with your Samsung's camera, or open the link — either downloads the `.apk` directly to your phone.

## Installing it

Your phone will likely warn "install blocked" the first time — tap through to **allow installing from this source** (usually your browser or file manager app), then install.

## After it's installed

Open the app from your home screen icon (not Expo Go). Test the full flow: arm, wait for it to ring, sound + vibration, movement, camera scan, PIN snooze, dismissal. Report back what works and what doesn't, and we'll move to 4F.

# bash: eas: command not found

`eas-cli` isn't installed yet — you need that first.

## Install it

```bash
npm install -g eas-cli
```

Wait for it to finish, then verify:

```bash
eas --version
```

If that shows a version number, proceed with the login/build steps from before.

## If the install fails or `eas` is still not found

Codespaces sometimes has permission issues with global npm installs. Use `npx` instead for every command — same result, no global install needed:

```bash
npx eas-cli login
npx eas-cli build:configure
npx eas-cli build --platform android --profile preview
```

Just replace `eas` with `npx eas-cli` in every command going forward if the global one doesn't work. Try the plain `npm install -g eas-cli` first, though — if it succeeds, you can keep typing the shorter `eas` command for everything after.

# npm worked and got eas version tried logina nd it gave a link to approve i approved but it later said This site can’t be reached

localhost refused to connect.
and also the console it not moving forward its stuck

That's a classic Codespaces problem: `eas login`'s browser approval tries to redirect back to `localhost` on your *local machine*, but the CLI is actually running inside the cloud container — so your browser can't reach that "localhost." This isn't something you can fix by retrying; you need a different login method entirely.

## Use a personal access token instead

This is the standard headless/remote-environment login method — no browser redirect involved.

### 1. Create a token

1. In any browser tab, go to `expo.dev` and log in (or sign up if you haven't).
2. Click your profile icon → **Account settings**.
3. Go to **Access tokens**.
4. Click **Create token**, give it any name (e.g. `codespaces`), and copy the token value shown — it's only shown once.

### 2. Use it in Codespaces

In your Codespaces terminal, first cancel the stuck login:

```text
Ctrl + C
```

Then set the token as an environment variable:

```bash
export EXPO_TOKEN=paste_your_token_here
```

Verify it worked:

```bash
eas whoami
```

It should print your Expo username — no browser needed at all.

## Then continue as before

```bash
eas build:configure
```

Select Android when prompted.

Check `eas.json`'s `preview` profile has `"buildType": "apk"` as I described earlier, then:

```bash
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```


## One thing to know

That `export EXPO_TOKEN=...` only lasts for this terminal session. If you close Codespaces and come back later, you'll need to run that `export` line again before using any `eas` command. That's fine for now — we're doing the build in this session.

Run `eas whoami` and confirm it shows your username before moving on.

That's expected and correct — it just confirms the token-based login worked. `eas whoami` shows tokens as a "robot" identity (Expo's term for machine/CI-based authentication, as opposed to an interactive browser login), which is exactly what a personal access token gives you. You're logged in.

## Continue

```bash
eas build:configure
```

Select **Android** when prompted, and accept the detected package name (`com.rainbowblurry.wakeupnative`).

Then open the new `eas.json` and confirm the `preview` profile has:

```json
"preview": {
  "distribution": "internal",
  "android": {
    "buildType": "apk"
  }
}
```

Add `"buildType": "apk"` if it's missing, save, then build:

```bash
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```

# EAS project not configured.

EAS project not configured. A robot access token cannot configure it interactively. Run one of the following, then re-run this command:

To link an existing project:

eas init --id \<project-id> --non-interactive

To create a new project:

eas init --account \<account-name> --non-interactive

Accounts you can create projects in: rainbowblurrys-team

Alternatively, set the "owner" field in your app config.

Robot tokens can't do the interactive "create or link project" step — this needs to be done explicitly and non-interactively, exactly as the message says.

## Create a new EAS project

```bash
eas init --account rainbowblurrys-team --non-interactive
```

This creates a new EAS project tied to your account and links it to `alarmG-native`, writing a `projectId` into your `app.json`/`app.config` automatically.

## Then continue

```bash
eas build:configure
```

If that still complains about interactivity, skip it — `eas init` alone may already have set up everything needed. Try the build directly:

```bash
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```

If `eas.json` doesn't exist yet (since `build:configure` may not have completed earlier), create it manually. Check first:

```bash
cat eas.json
```

If that says "No such file," create `eas.json` in the project root with this content:

```json
{
  "cli": {
    "version": ">= 12.0.0",
    "appVersionSource": "remote"
  },
  "build": {
    "preview": {
      "distribution": "internal",
      "android": {
        "buildType": "apk"
      }
    },
    "production": {
      "autoIncrement": true
    }
  },
  "submit": {
    "production": {}
  }
}
```

Save it, then run:

```bash
eas build --platform android --profile preview
```

Paste whatever output comes next — either it starts uploading and building, or it asks another one-time setup question that I'll walk you through.

# make sure i can do it as a build for ios as well so my friednd can tst as well so do i click andrioid or ios or all

For today, choose **Android only**. iOS needs a completely different (and paid) setup — build only what will actually work right now, and add iOS later once you're ready for that cost.

## Why iOS is different

Android sideloading (what we're doing) is free — you build an APK and just install it directly on any Android phone, no store, no account fees.

iOS has no equivalent free sideloading path. To get a build onto your friend's iPhone, you need one of:

```text
Apple Developer Program membership — $99/year
+ register your friend's device UDID
+ EAS build with iOS ad-hoc distribution

OR

TestFlight — still requires the same $99/year Apple Developer account,
plus App Store Connect setup
```

There's no way around the \$99/year for real iPhone testing outside Expo Go. (Testing in Expo Go itself is free on iOS, but that's not the same as installing your own built app.)

## What to select right now

Whenever you see a platform prompt (in `eas init`, `eas build:configure`, or `eas build`), choose:

```text
Android
```

Not "All." Selecting iOS now will just fail or get stuck asking for Apple credentials you don't have yet.

## For your friend to test today

If your friend has an **Android phone**, they can install the same APK you're building — just share the download link or the file directly (e.g. via Google Drive, WhatsApp, email). No extra work needed on your end.

If your friend has an **iPhone**, the only free option today is: have them install **Expo Go** and scan your Expo tunnel QR code (same as you've been doing) — but that only works while your Codespaces session is actively running `expo start --tunnel`, not as an installed standalone app.

## Decide iOS later

Once the Android APK is working end-to-end and you're actually ready to invest in iOS distribution, come back and we'll set up:

```text
Apple Developer Program enrollment
eas build --platform ios
Device registration or TestFlight
```

For now: **Android only**, continue with the build command from before.

# i guess it did the build but now ive lost itas code spaces restarted

Don't worry — the build itself runs entirely on Expo's cloud servers, not in your Codespaces terminal. Once you ran `eas build`, it was already uploaded and building remotely. Your Codespaces restarting only killed the **local terminal view** of it, not the actual build.

## Easiest recovery: check the website directly

1. Go to `expo.dev` in any browser tab.
2. Log in with your account (the one tied to `rainbowblurrys-team`).
3. Open your project — it should be listed under your account/team.
4. Click the **Builds** tab.
5. You'll see the build listed with a status: `in progress`, `finished`, or `errored`.
6. If it's finished, there's a **Download** button right there — get the APK straight from the website, no CLI needed.

## If you'd rather check from Codespaces

Reopen your Codespace (same repo, same terminal), then re-authenticate since the environment variable doesn't survive a restart:

```bash
export EXPO_TOKEN=paste_your_token_here
eas whoami
```

Then check build status:

```bash
eas build:list
```

This lists your recent builds with their status and a link to each.

## What to do next

- If the build shows **finished**: download the APK from the website or the link `eas build:list` gives you, then install it on your Samsung as before.
- If it shows **errored**: paste the error here and I'll fix whatever caused it.
- If it's still **in progress**: just wait — it continues regardless of whether your Codespaces terminal is open.

Check the website first — it's the fastest way to see exactly where things stand.

## Important — stay connected until it says "Build finished"

The upload phase (few minutes) needs your Codespaces session to stay active. Once it clears the upload and starts showing cloud build progress logs, it's safer — but to avoid a repeat, just don't close the tab, let your device sleep, or navigate away until you see one of:

```text
✅ Build finished
```

or a download link/QR code printed in the terminal.

The whole thing (upload + cloud build) typically takes 10–20 minutes. Keep the Codespaces browser tab open and active the entire time this time.

## Verify it's actually queued

Once you see the upload complete and it starts printing build logs, open a second check in another browser tab: go to `expo.dev` → your project → **Builds** tab. You should now see an entry with status `in progress`. That confirms it's safely on Expo's servers — from that point on, even if Codespaces disconnects, the build itself will continue and finish on their side.

Run it now and paste the first bit of output so I can confirm it's progressing correctly this time.

# Resolved "preview" environment for the build. Learn more

No environment variables with visibility "Plain text" and "Sensitive" found for the "preview" environment on EAS.

✔ Using remote Android credentials (Expo server)
? Generate a new Android Keystore? › (Y/n)

Say **Yes** (Y).

## What to type

```text
Y
```

then press Enter.

## Why

This is asking whether EAS should generate and store a signing keystore for your app on their servers. Since this is your first Android build for this project, you don't have one yet — you need EAS to create it.

Important to understand: **this keystore becomes permanent for this app.** Every future build of `alarmG-native` (including if you ever publish to the Play Store later) must be signed with the same keystore, or Android will refuse to let you update an already-installed version. EAS stores and manages it for you automatically on their servers, so you generally don't need to manually back it up — but don't run this "generate new keystore" step again for this same project later, or you'll create a second, incompatible one.

Confirm with `Y` and let it continue — it should proceed straight into the actual cloud build queue after this.
